### Question 101: Pancreatic Islet Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of islet amyloid deposition in the pancreas, specifically its composition (IAPP/amylin), its association with type 2 diabetes mellitus, and the histological appearance of the deposits. (胰岛淀粉样蛋白沉积，其成分（IAPP/淀粉样多肽），与2型糖尿病的关系，以及沉积物的组织学外观。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a 25-year history of poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department complaining of severe, crushing chest pain radiating to his left arm. He has a history of hypertension and hyperlipidemia. On arrival, his blood pressure is 160/100 mmHg, heart rate is 110 bpm, and respiratory rate is 24 breaths/min. An ECG shows ST-segment elevation in leads II, III, and aVF. He is diagnosed with an acute inferior wall myocardial infarction and undergoes emergent coronary angiography, revealing severe three-vessel coronary artery disease. Despite aggressive medical management, he dies within 24 hours. Autopsy is performed. Histological examination of the pancreas reveals extensive replacement of the islets of Langerhans by dense, amorphous, pink, acellular extracellular material that stains positively with Congo red and exhibits apple-green birefringence under polarized light. This material is derived from a peptide co-secreted with insulin.

Which of the following molecules is primarily responsible for the formation of these pancreatic islet deposits?

(A) Insulin
(B) Glucagon
(C) Somatostatin
(D) Islet amyloid polypeptide (IAPP) / Amylin
(E) Proinsulin

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with a long-standing (25 years) history of type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM). This immediately points towards pancreatic pathology related to chronic hyperglycemia and insulin resistance/secretion issues.
- **Cause of Death:** Acute myocardial infarction (MI) secondary to severe coronary artery disease (CAD). While the MI is the immediate cause of death, the autopsy findings are the focus of the question. The patient's history of T2DM is a major risk factor for CAD.
- **Autopsy Finding:** Histological examination of the pancreas shows "extensive replacement of the islets of Langerhans by dense, amorphous, pink, acellular extracellular material". This describes amyloid deposition within the islets.
- **Staining Properties:** The material "stains positively with Congo red and exhibits apple-green birefringence under polarized light". This is the classic staining pattern for amyloid.
- **Origin of Material:** The material is described as being "derived from a peptide co-secreted with insulin". This is the crucial clue linking the deposit to beta-cell function.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus:** Characterized by insulin resistance and progressive beta-cell dysfunction. Initially, beta-cells compensate by increasing insulin secretion (hyperinsulinemia).
- **Beta-Cell Stress:** Chronic hypersecretion of insulin places significant stress on the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) and Golgi apparatus of beta-cells.
- **IAPP/Amylin:** Islet amyloid polypeptide (IAPP), also known as amylin, is a peptide hormone co-secreted with insulin from pancreatic beta-cells in response to glucose stimulation. It is synthesized as a precursor molecule (pro-IAPP) and processed into the active form.
- **Misfolding and Aggregation:** Under conditions of chronic beta-cell stress and hypersecretion (common in T2DM), IAPP can misfold and aggregate. These aggregates initially form small oligomers and fibrils.
- **Amyloid Formation:** Over time, these fibrils deposit extracellularly within the islets of Langerhans, forming amyloid plaques. These plaques replace normal islet tissue, including beta-cells, contributing to beta-cell loss and worsening diabetes.
- **Histology:** IAPP amyloid deposits appear as dense, amorphous, eosinophilic (pink on H&E) extracellular material. They stain positively with Congo red, exhibiting characteristic apple-green birefringence under polarized light, confirming their amyloid nature.
- **Other Hormones:** Insulin itself can also aggregate but is less prone to forming amyloid deposits than IAPP. Glucagon (secreted by alpha-cells), somatostatin (secreted by delta-cells), and proinsulin (precursor to insulin) are not the primary components of islet amyloid in T2DM.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Insulin:** While insulin is secreted by beta-cells and can aggregate, it is not the primary component of islet amyloid deposits in T2DM. Insulin aggregates are more commonly seen in insulinomas or after exogenous insulin administration, and they typically do not exhibit the same strong Congo red positivity or characteristic birefringence as IAPP amyloid. The vignette specifically mentions a co-secreted peptide.
- (B) **Glucagon:** Glucagon is secreted by alpha-cells, not beta-cells. Alpha-cells are located within the islets but are not the source of the amyloid deposits described, which are associated with beta-cell dysfunction and hypersecretion. Glucagon itself is not known to form amyloid deposits in the pancreas.
- (C) **Somatostatin:** Somatostatin is secreted by delta-cells, also located within the islets. Like glucagon, it is not the primary component of islet amyloid. Somatostatinomas can occur, but amyloid deposition is not a characteristic feature.
- (E) **Proinsulin:** Proinsulin is the precursor to insulin, synthesized and stored in beta-cells. While abnormal processing or accumulation of proinsulin can occur in certain conditions (like insulinoma or specific genetic defects), it is not the main component of the amyloid plaques found in the islets of patients with long-standing T2DM. The amyloid is derived from a distinct peptide co-secreted with insulin.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Islet amyloid deposition, composed primarily of misfolded IAPP/amylin, is a characteristic pathological finding in the pancreas of patients with long-standing type 2 diabetes mellitus. This deposition contributes to beta-cell dysfunction and loss. The deposits stain positive with Congo red and show apple-green birefringence.
- **Differentials:** Consider other causes of amyloidosis (systemic AL, AA, ATTR), but the location (pancreatic islets), staining characteristics, and association with T2DM strongly point to IAPP amyloid. Also, differentiate from other islet cell tumors or inflammatory conditions.

### Question 102: Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC) is characterized by the deposition of amyloid in the stroma, which is derived from procalcitonin (A-Cal), a precursor peptide for calcitonin. This amyloid deposition is a key histological feature used in diagnosis. (髓样甲状腺癌的特点是在间质中沉积淀粉样物，该淀粉样物来源于降钙素的前体多肽原钙调素 (A-Cal)，这是诊断中的一个关键组织学特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman presents to her primary care physician complaining of a rapidly enlarging mass in the anterior neck. She reports no significant past medical history, including no history of radiation exposure. Physical examination reveals a firm, solitary 3 cm nodule in the right lobe of the thyroid gland, along with palpable, firm lymph nodes in the right cervical region. Vital signs are within normal limits. Fine needle aspiration (FNA) biopsy of the thyroid nodule is suspicious for malignancy. Serum calcitonin levels are markedly elevated at 1,200 pg/mL (normal < 10 pg/mL). Genetic testing reveals a germline mutation in the RET proto-oncogene, consistent with Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2A (MEN 2A). Following total thyroidectomy and central neck dissection, histological examination of the tumor shows nests of polygonal cells with granular cytoplasm infiltrating the thyroid parenchyma and lymph nodes. The tumor cells are separated by a large amount of pale pink, amorphous extracellular material that stains positive with Congo red and shows apple-green birefringence under polarized light. Immunohistochemical staining for calcitonin is strongly positive within the tumor cells. Which of the following substances is the primary component of the characteristic extracellular amyloid deposits seen in this patient's tumor?

(A) Serum Amyloid A (SAA) protein
(B) Immunoglobulin light chains (AL amyloid)
(C) Transthyretin (ATTR amyloid)
(D) Procalcitonin (A-Cal)
(E) Amyloid-beta (Aβ) peptide

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old woman. Age is relevant as MTC can occur at various ages, but familial forms (like MEN 2A) often present earlier.
*   **Presenting Complaint:** Rapidly enlarging neck mass. Suggests a thyroid nodule or lymphadenopathy.
*   **Physical Exam:** Firm, solitary thyroid nodule (3 cm) and palpable cervical lymphadenopathy. Points towards a malignant thyroid process with regional spread.
*   **Lab Finding:** Markedly elevated serum calcitonin (1,200 pg/mL). Calcitonin is produced by parafollicular C-cells of the thyroid. High levels are highly specific for medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC).
*   **Genetic Finding:** Germline RET proto-oncogene mutation (MEN 2A). Confirms the diagnosis of familial MTC, strongly associated with MEN 2A syndrome.
*   **Histopathology:** Total thyroidectomy reveals nests of polygonal cells (characteristic of MTC) infiltrating parenchyma and lymph nodes. Crucially, there is abundant extracellular amyloid stroma. Congo red staining with apple-green birefringence confirms amyloid deposition. Immunohistochemistry shows calcitonin positivity in tumor cells.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the primary component of the characteristic extracellular amyloid deposits in MTC.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
Medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC) arises from the parafollicular C-cells of the thyroid gland, which normally produce calcitonin. In MTC, these cells proliferate abnormally and secrete excessive amounts of calcitonin, leading to elevated serum levels. The tumor cells also process procalcitonin (also known as calcitonin precursor or A-Cal) into calcitonin. Unprocessed procalcitonin, along with fragments of calcitonin, can misfold and aggregate extracellularly within the tumor stroma, forming amyloid fibrils. These fibrils deposit as characteristic amyloid masses, which are a hallmark histological feature of MTC. Congo red staining demonstrates the amyloid nature of these deposits, showing pink staining with apple-green birefringence under polarized light. Therefore, the amyloid in MTC is derived from procalcitonin (A-Cal).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Serum Amyloid A (SAA) protein:** SAA is an acute-phase reactant protein synthesized by the liver. Amyloid deposits composed primarily of SAA are associated with secondary (reactive) systemic amyloidosis, often seen in chronic inflammatory conditions like rheumatoid arthritis or chronic infections. This is unrelated to MTC.
- (B) **Immunoglobulin light chains (AL amyloid):** AL amyloidosis is a form of primary systemic amyloidosis caused by the deposition of misfolded monoclonal immunoglobulin light chains produced by a plasma cell dyscrasia (e.g., multiple myeloma). While AL amyloid can deposit in various organs, it is not the characteristic amyloid found in MTC.
- (C) **Transthyretin (ATTR amyloid):** Transthyretin (formerly prealbumin) is a protein primarily synthesized by the liver. Misfolded transthyretin can form amyloid fibrils, leading to hereditary (ATTRv) or wild-type (ATTRwt) amyloidosis. ATTR amyloidosis typically affects the heart, peripheral nerves, and kidneys, not the thyroid in the manner described.
- (E) **Amyloid-beta (Aβ) peptide:** Amyloid-beta peptide is derived from the amyloid precursor protein (APP) and is the primary component of amyloid plaques found in the brains of patients with Alzheimer's disease. It is not associated with MTC.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Medullary thyroid carcinoma is characterized by the production of calcitonin by malignant C-cells and the deposition of amyloid derived from procalcitonin (A-Cal) in the tumor stroma. This amyloid is Congo red positive with apple-green birefringence. Key differentials for thyroid nodules include follicular adenoma/carcinoma, papillary thyroid carcinoma, and anaplastic thyroid carcinoma. MTC is distinguished by its C-cell origin, high calcitonin levels, and characteristic amyloid deposition.

### Question 103: Renal Amyloidosis: Glomerular Amyloid & Nephrotic Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Amyloid deposition in the glomeruli (specifically AL amyloidosis) leads to nephrotic syndrome by disrupting the glomerular filtration barrier, characterized by massive proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, edema, and specific histological findings (Congo red staining with apple-green birefringence).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of rapidly worsening bilateral leg edema over the past month. He reports feeling fatigued and has noticed a significant decrease in urine output. His past medical history is unremarkable except for hypertension, well-controlled with lisinopril. On physical examination, he is found to be hypertensive (160/95 mmHg) with significant pitting edema extending up to his thighs. Laboratory investigations reveal a 24-hour urine protein excretion of 9.2 grams, serum albumin of 1.8 g/dL, and elevated serum creatinine (2.5 mg/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse expansion of the mesangium and marked thickening of the glomerular capillary basement membranes. Immunofluorescence is negative for immune complex deposition. Congo red staining reveals extensive deposition of homogenous, amorphous, acellular material throughout the glomeruli, which exhibits apple-green birefringence under cross-polarized light. Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism causing this patient's nephrotic syndrome?

(A) Immune complex deposition leading to complement activation and glomerular injury.
(B) Fibril formation from misfolded transthyretin (TTR) protein, primarily affecting the renal tubules and interstitium.
(C) Deposition of misfolded immunoglobulin light chains forming fibrils that disrupt the glomerular filtration barrier.
(D) Accumulation of oxidized lipids and foam cells within the glomerular capillaries, leading to podocyte effacement.
(E) Genetic mutations affecting collagen IV synthesis, resulting in Alport syndrome and glomerular basement membrane splitting.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male. Age is relevant as amyloidosis incidence increases with age.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Rapidly worsening bilateral leg edema, fatigue, decreased urine output. These are classic signs of nephrotic syndrome (edema due to low oncotic pressure) and potential renal dysfunction.
- **Past Medical History:** Hypertension (controlled). Not directly causative but common comorbidity.
- **Physical Exam:** Hypertension (160/95 mmHg), significant pitting edema. Consistent with nephrotic syndrome and potential underlying renal disease.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - 24-hour urine protein: 9.2 grams (Normal < 0.15 g/day). This defines nephrotic-range proteinuria (>3.5 g/day).
    - Serum albumin: 1.8 g/dL (Normal ~3.5-5.0 g/dL). Hypoalbuminemia is a hallmark of nephrotic syndrome due to urinary protein loss.
    - Serum creatinine: 2.5 mg/dL (Normal ~0.7-1.3 mg/dL). Indicates impaired renal function (acute kidney injury or chronic kidney disease).
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - Light Microscopy: Diffuse mesangial expansion, marked thickening of glomerular capillary basement membranes. Suggests glomerular disease affecting the filtration barrier.
    - Immunofluorescence: Negative for immune complexes. Rules out diseases like lupus nephritis, post-infectious glomerulonephritis, or IgA nephropathy.
    - Congo Red Staining: Extensive homogenous, amorphous, acellular material deposition in glomeruli. This is characteristic of amyloid deposition.
    - Cross-Polarized Light: Apple-green birefringence. This is pathognomonic for amyloid.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *underlying mechanism* causing the nephrotic syndrome. The findings strongly point to renal amyloidosis, specifically affecting the glomeruli. The negative immunofluorescence and characteristic Congo red staining with birefringence confirm amyloid deposition. The massive proteinuria and hypoalbuminemia define nephrotic syndrome. The key is to identify the *type* of amyloid and its mechanism. The patient's age and presentation are most consistent with AL amyloidosis (primary amyloidosis), derived from monoclonal immunoglobulin light chains.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **Amyloidosis:** A group of diseases characterized by the extracellular deposition of insoluble fibrillar proteins (amyloid) in various tissues and organs.
- **Renal Amyloidosis:** Kidneys are frequently affected, particularly the glomeruli.
- **Types of Amyloid:**
    - **AL (Primary) Amyloidosis:** Derived from monoclonal immunoglobulin light chains (kappa or lambda) produced by a plasma cell dyscrasia (e.g., multiple myeloma, monoclonal gammopathy of undetermined significance - MGUS). This is the most common type causing nephrotic syndrome in adults.
    - **AA (Secondary) Amyloidosis:** Derived from serum amyloid A (SAA), an acute-phase reactant. Associated with chronic inflammatory conditions (e.g., rheumatoid arthritis, chronic infections). Typically affects kidneys, liver, spleen, adrenals.
    - **ATTR (Transthyretin) Amyloidosis:** Derived from misfolded transthyretin protein. Can be hereditary (ATTRv) or wild-type (ATTRwt, senile systemic amyloidosis). Affects heart, nerves, kidneys (less commonly causes nephrotic syndrome compared to AL).
    - **Other types:** Less common (e.g., Aβ amyloid in Alzheimer's, islet amyloid polypeptide in type 2 diabetes).
- **Mechanism in this Case (AL Amyloidosis):**
    1. **Plasma Cell Dyscrasia:** A clonal population of plasma cells produces excessive amounts of monoclonal immunoglobulin light chains (e.g., lambda6).
    2. **Misfolding and Aggregation:** These light chains are prone to misfolding and aggregation into soluble oligomers and then insoluble fibrils.
    3. **Deposition:** These fibrils deposit extracellularly in various organs, including the kidney.
    4. **Glomerular Deposition:** In the glomeruli, amyloid fibrils deposit primarily in the mesangium and the walls of glomerular capillaries (subendothelial and sometimes subepithelial).
    5. **Disruption of Filtration Barrier:** The amyloid deposits physically expand the mesangium and thicken the capillary walls, distorting the glomerular architecture and disrupting the normal structure of the glomerular filtration barrier (GBM, podocytes, mesangial cells).
    6. **Proteinuria:** This disruption leads to increased permeability of the glomerular capillaries to plasma proteins, particularly albumin, resulting in massive proteinuria (>3.5 g/day), characteristic of nephrotic syndrome.
    7. **Nephrotic Syndrome:** The massive loss of protein (especially albumin) in the urine leads to hypoalbuminemia (<3.0 g/dL), decreased plasma oncotic pressure, fluid shift into the interstitial space (edema), and hyperlipidemia (due to increased hepatic lipoprotein synthesis).
    8. **Renal Failure:** Progressive amyloid deposition can lead to glomerulosclerosis, tubular atrophy, and interstitial fibrosis, eventually causing chronic kidney disease and renal failure (as indicated by the elevated creatinine in this case).
- **Histopathology Confirmation:** Congo red staining highlights the amyloid deposits, and apple-green birefringence under polarized light is pathognomonic.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Immune complex deposition leading to complement activation and glomerular injury.** This mechanism is characteristic of diseases like lupus nephritis, post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis, membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN), and IgA nephropathy. These conditions typically show granular deposits on immunofluorescence (IgG, IgM, IgA, C3) and often have features like mesangial proliferation, endocapillary proliferation, or crescent formation. This patient has *negative* immunofluorescence, ruling out immune complex-mediated glomerular injury.
- (B): **Fibril formation from misfolded transthyretin (TTR) protein, primarily affecting the renal tubules and interstitium.** This describes ATTR amyloidosis. While ATTR can affect the kidneys, it typically causes tubular and interstitial deposition rather than predominantly glomerular deposition leading to nephrotic syndrome. ATTR amyloidosis

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Renal Amyloidosis: Tubular & Vascular Involvement
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Amyloidosis can affect multiple renal compartments (glomeruli, tubules, interstitium, vessels), leading to diverse clinical manifestations including nephrotic syndrome, renal tubular acidosis (RTA), and renal insufficiency. The specific pattern of deposition dictates the clinical presentation. (淀粉样变性可影响肾脏多个区室（肾小球、肾小管、间质、血管），导致多种临床表现，包括肾病综合征、肾小管性酸中毒 (RTA) 和肾功能不全。沉积的具体模式决定了临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old woman with a known history of multiple myeloma presents to her primary care physician complaining of excessive thirst (polydipsia) and frequent urination, especially at night (nocturia), for the past 6 months. She also reports increasing fatigue. Her past medical history is significant for hypertension managed with lisinopril and hypercalcemia related to her myeloma. Laboratory investigations reveal hyperkalemia (serum K+ 6.2 mEq/L), metabolic acidosis (serum HCO3- 14 mEq/L) with a normal anion gap, and elevated serum creatinine (2.1 mg/dL). Urinalysis shows no proteinuria. A renal biopsy is performed, revealing amorphous, eosinophilic deposits in the glomeruli, along the basement membranes of the proximal and distal convoluted tubules, and within the media of interlobular arteries. Congo red staining confirms the presence of amyloid, and immunohistochemistry shows lambda light chain restriction. Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms BEST explains the patient's polyuria, nocturia, and hyperkalemia?

(A) Glomerular basement membrane thickening leading to impaired filtration of water and electrolytes, causing fluid retention and hyperkalemia due to decreased distal tubular secretion.
(B) Amyloid deposition within the glomerular mesangium causing podocyte effacement and nephrotic syndrome, leading to massive proteinuria and subsequent electrolyte imbalances.
(C) Amyloid deposition along tubular basement membranes impairing the function of the distal convoluted tubule and collecting duct, leading to impaired sodium reabsorption and potassium secretion, resulting in salt wasting and hyperkalemia.
(D) Amyloid deposition within the media of interlobular arteries causing vascular ischemia and tubular atrophy, leading to impaired concentrating ability and decreased glomerular filtration rate (GFR), resulting in hyperkalemia due to reduced distal tubular flow.
(E) Amyloid deposition in the renal interstitium causing interstitial fibrosis and tubular obstruction, leading to impaired tubular function and decreased GFR, resulting in metabolic acidosis and hyperkalemia.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62yo female with multiple myeloma (a known cause of AL amyloidosis).
- **Symptoms:** Polyuria, nocturia (suggesting impaired concentrating ability), fatigue (non-specific, but common in renal disease).
- **Labs:** Hyperkalemia (K+ 6.2 mEq/L), metabolic acidosis (HCO3- 14 mEq/L, normal anion gap), elevated creatinine (2.1 mg/dL, indicating renal insufficiency). Absence of proteinuria is notable.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Amyloid deposition in glomeruli, *tubular basement membranes*, and *interlobular arteries*. Congo red positive, lambda light chain restricted (confirms AL amyloidosis).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining polyuria, nocturia, and hyperkalemia.

The key findings are the multisite amyloid deposition (glomeruli, tubules, arteries) in a patient with AL amyloidosis, leading to impaired concentrating ability (polyuria, nocturia), hyperkalemia, metabolic acidosis, and renal insufficiency, *without significant proteinuria*. This pattern points towards tubular and vascular involvement as the primary drivers of the specific symptoms asked about.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **Amyloidosis Pathogenesis:** AL amyloidosis is caused by the deposition of misfolded immunoglobulin light chains (in this case, lambda) produced by clonal plasma cells (multiple myeloma). These light chains form amyloid fibrils that deposit extracellularly in various organs, including the kidneys.
- **Renal Deposition Sites:** In AL amyloidosis, the kidneys are frequently involved. Deposition can occur in the glomeruli (leading to nephrotic syndrome if extensive), the tubules (interstitial and basement membranes), and the blood vessels (arteries and arterioles).
- **Tubular Involvement:** Deposition along tubular basement membranes and within the interstitium disrupts tubular function. This can lead to impaired reabsorption (e.g., bicarbonate, glucose, amino acids) and secretion (e.g., potassium). Impaired function of the distal tubule and collecting duct, specifically affecting aquaporin channels and sodium/potassium channels, leads to impaired water reabsorption (causing polyuria and nocturia) and impaired potassium secretion (contributing to hyperkalemia). This specific pattern is often referred to as Renal Tubular Acidosis (RTA) type IV, characterized by hyperkalemia and normal anion gap metabolic acidosis due to impaired ammoniagenesis and aldosterone effect.
- **Vascular Involvement:** Deposition within the media of renal arteries (arteriolosclerosis) causes narrowing of the vessel lumen, leading to reduced blood flow and ischemia to the glomeruli and tubules. This contributes to tubular atrophy, interstitial fibrosis, and a decline in GFR. Reduced GFR can worsen hyperkalemia by decreasing the overall filtration and excretion of potassium. Ischemia also impairs tubular function, further contributing to the concentrating defect and electrolyte imbalances.
- **Glomerular Involvement:** While amyloid deposition in glomeruli can cause nephrotic syndrome (heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, edema), this patient notably lacks significant proteinuria. This suggests that glomerular involvement, while present, is not the dominant feature causing the specific symptoms queried (polyuria, nocturia, hyperkalemia).
- **Connecting to the Question:** The patient's polyuria and nocturia are due to impaired concentrating ability, primarily caused by dysfunction of the distal tubules and collecting ducts due to amyloid deposition. The hyperkalemia results from both impaired tubular secretion of potassium (due to tubular damage) and reduced GFR (due to vascular ischemia and tubular atrophy), which decreases overall potassium excretion. Therefore, the combination of tubular dysfunction (impairing secretion) and vascular ischemia (reducing GFR and tubular perfusion) best explains the clinical picture. Option (D) correctly identifies the vascular ischemia leading to impaired concentrating ability and decreased GFR as the key mechanism for hyperkalemia in this context.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Glomerular basement membrane thickening is characteristic of diabetic nephropathy or membranous nephropathy, not typically the primary feature of amyloidosis causing these symptoms. While amyloid can deposit in glomeruli, it usually leads to nephrotic syndrome (proteinuria), which is absent here. Impaired filtration *due to glomerular disease* typically causes fluid retention (edema), not polyuria. Hyperkalemia from decreased distal secretion is possible with severe glomerular disease, but the primary issue here is tubular and vascular damage.
- (B): Amyloid deposition in the mesangium *can* cause nephrotic syndrome. However, this patient lacks significant proteinuria. Nephrotic syndrome leads to fluid retention and edema, not typically polyuria/nocturia (unless secondary to osmotic diuresis from glucose or mannitol, which is not indicated here). While electrolyte imbalances can occur, the specific combination of polyuria, nocturia, and hyperkalemia without proteinuria points away from nephrotic syndrome as the primary explanation.
- (C): Amyloid deposition along tubular basement membranes *does* impair tubular function, leading to impaired sodium reabsorption and potassium secretion. This contributes to salt wasting and hyperkalemia (consistent with RTA type IV). However, this option *only* focuses on tubular deposition and doesn't account for the significant vascular deposition seen on biopsy, which contributes substantially to the reduced GFR and impaired tubular perfusion, worsening the concentrating defect and hyperkalemia. Option (D) provides a more complete picture incorporating both tubular and vascular damage.
- (E): Amyloid deposition in the interstitium causes interstitial fibrosis and tubular obstruction. This contributes to renal insufficiency (decreased GFR) and can impair tubular function, leading to metabolic acidosis and hyperkal
